Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, even when I’m trying to.

I’m a construction manager, so I’m in safety briefings and plan reviews all the time, and I’ll go in telling myself, like, “Okay, pay attention, this matters.” And I do mean to. But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.

It’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’m trying hard to focus, especially on safety stuff, but my mind just keeps jumping off track. That’s the main reason I’m here.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s definitely worst and most obvious at work, because that’s where it has consequences right away, you know? Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.

That’s the part that bothers me. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.

But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’m trying hard to focus, especially on safety stuff, but my mind just keeps jumping off track. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.

And then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.

manifestation: Waiting for my turn on a call, at the permit office, or in a line drives me nuts, and I’m checking the time, shifting around, and fighting the urge to push ahead.\n- key: S018\n  name: Interrupting_Intruding\n  description: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n  manifestation: I’ll walk up on two subcontractors talking or someone working through paperwork and jump right in with my own point before I’ve been asked, and it rubs people the wrong way.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, talked out of turn, lost homework, and rushed through directions before I really understood them.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has gotten bad enough that missed follow-ups and poorly organized site coordination led to formal concern from my employer, and at home my wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I said I would handle.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: It has been seriously disrupting my job for about two years and two months, ever since the construction schedule changed and the hours got more chaotic.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / construction_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S002\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, even when I’m trying to.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so I’m in safety briefings and plan reviews all the time, and I’ll go in telling myself, like, “Okay, pay attention, this matters.” And I do mean to. But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’m trying hard to focus, especially on safety stuff, but my mind just keeps jumping off track. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only at work or do you find it happening in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worst and most obvious at work, because that’s where it has consequences right away, you know? Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're not at work, like when you're reading, watching TV, or having conversations with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is hard for me, especially in meetings. Like, if everybody else can just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy who’s up at some point — getting coffee, stepping out to “check something,” walking over to look at a printout, anything that gives me a reason to move.

Sometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until afterward, but other times I’m sitting there and it feels like I’ve got to get up or I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll make it look like I’m being productive, like, “Oh, I’m just going to check on that,” or “Let me grab something,” but honestly a lot of it is just needing to not be stuck in the chair.

Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me.   {
    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do, especially with talking. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.

And then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like I cut them off, gave this whole answer, and then they say, “No, what I meant was…” or they finish the sentence and it’s not exactly what I thought. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out fast, like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.

It happens enough that people will pause and look at me, or I’ll have to say, “Sorry, go ahead, finish what you were saying.” I hate that, because I do want to listen, but if I think I know the answer I kind of fire it off too soon.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.

I can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. But as far as anxiety or constant worry, I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.

Yeah, quiet stuff is hard for me if I’m supposed to just sit there. The clearest place it shows up is meetings, honestly. Everybody else can sit through the whole thing, and I’m the one standing up at some point, walking over to the coffee, stepping outside like I need to check something, or making some excuse to move around.

I’ll try to make it look normal, like, “I’m just grabbing that printout,” or “Let me go check on that real quick,” but a lot of the time it’s not really about the coffee or the paper or whatever. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. I start feeling stuck, and then I’m looking for a reason to get up.

Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.

So yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, even when I’m trying to.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so I’m in safety briefings and plan reviews all the time, and I’ll go in telling myself, like, “Okay, pay attention, this matters.” And I do mean to. But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’m trying hard to focus, especially on safety stuff, but my mind just keeps jumping off track. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only at work or do you find it happening in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worst and most obvious at work, because that’s where it has consequences right away, you know? Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're not at work, like when you're reading, watching TV, or having conversations with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is hard for me, especially in meetings. Like, if everybody else can just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy who’s up at some point — getting coffee, stepping out to “check something,” walking over to look at a printout, anything that gives me a reason to move.\n\nSometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until afterward, but other times I’m sitting there and it feels like I’ve got to get up or I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll make it look like I’m being productive, like, “Oh, I’m just going to check on that,” or “Let me grab something,” but honestly a lot of it is just needing to not be stuck in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially with talking. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like I cut them off, gave this whole answer, and then they say, “No, what I meant was…” or they finish the sentence and it’s not exactly what I thought. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out fast, like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nIt happens enough that people will pause and look at me, or I’ll have to say, “Sorry, go ahead, finish what you were saying.” I hate that, because I do want to listen, but if I think I know the answer I kind of fire it off too soon.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience feelings of anxiety, worry, or irritability?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nI can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. But as far as anxiety or constant worry, I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, quiet stuff is hard for me if I’m supposed to just sit there. The clearest place it shows up is meetings, honestly. Everybody else can sit through the whole thing, and I’m the one standing up at some point, walking over to the coffee, stepping outside like I need to check something, or making some excuse to move around.\n\nI’ll try to make it look normal, like, “I’m just grabbing that printout,” or “Let me go check on that real quick,” but a lot of the time it’s not really about the coffee or the paper or whatever. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. I start feeling stuck, and then I’m looking for a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.

Like, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.

Can you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, even when I’m trying to.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so I’m in safety briefings and plan reviews all the time, and I’ll go in telling myself, like, “Okay, pay attention, this matters.” And I do mean to. But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’m trying hard to focus, especially on safety stuff, but my mind just keeps jumping off track. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only at work or do you find it happening in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worst and most obvious at work, because that’s where it has consequences right away, you know? Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're not at work, like when you're reading, watching TV, or having conversations with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is hard for me, especially in meetings. Like, if everybody else can just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy who’s up at some point — getting coffee, stepping out to “check something,” walking over to look at a printout, anything that gives me a reason to move.\n\nSometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until afterward, but other times I’m sitting there and it feels like I’ve got to get up or I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll make it look like I’m being productive, like, “Oh, I’m just going to check on that,” or “Let me grab something,” but honestly a lot of it is just needing to not be stuck in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially with talking. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like I cut them off, gave this whole answer, and then they say, “No, what I meant was…” or they finish the sentence and it’s not exactly what I thought. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out fast, like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nIt happens enough that people will pause and look at me, or I’ll have to say, “Sorry, go ahead, finish what you were saying.” I hate that, because I do want to listen, but if I think I know the answer I kind of fire it off too soon.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience feelings of anxiety, worry, or irritability?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nI can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. But as far as anxiety or constant worry, I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, quiet stuff is hard for me if I’m supposed to just sit there. The clearest place it shows up is meetings, honestly. Everybody else can sit through the whole thing, and I’m the one standing up at some point, walking over to the coffee, stepping outside like I need to check something, or making some excuse to move around.\n\nI’ll try to make it look normal, like, “I’m just grabbing that printout,” or “Let me go check on that real quick,” but a lot of the time it’s not really about the coffee or the paper or whatever. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. I start feeling stuck, and then I’m looking for a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / construction_manager / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, talked out of turn, lost homework, and rushed through directions before I really understood them.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, this has been around as long as I can remember. It’s not like it showed up in adulthood or after I started working. Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents about it.

I remember — well, some of it I remember and some of it is from my parents repeating it over the years — but they’d get told I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always popping up or fidgeting around, and that I talked out of turn. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.

The directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, even when I’m trying to.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so I’m in safety briefings and plan reviews all the time, and I’ll go in telling myself, like, “Okay, pay attention, this matters.” And I do mean to. But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’m trying hard to focus, especially on safety stuff, but my mind just keeps jumping off track. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only at work or do you find it happening in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worst and most obvious at work, because that’s where it has consequences right away, you know? Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're not at work, like when you're reading, watching TV, or having conversations with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is hard for me, especially in meetings. Like, if everybody else can just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy who’s up at some point — getting coffee, stepping out to “check something,” walking over to look at a printout, anything that gives me a reason to move.\n\nSometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until afterward, but other times I’m sitting there and it feels like I’ve got to get up or I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll make it look like I’m being productive, like, “Oh, I’m just going to check on that,” or “Let me grab something,” but honestly a lot of it is just needing to not be stuck in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially with talking. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like I cut them off, gave this whole answer, and then they say, “No, what I meant was…” or they finish the sentence and it’s not exactly what I thought. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out fast, like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nIt happens enough that people will pause and look at me, or I’ll have to say, “Sorry, go ahead, finish what you were saying.” I hate that, because I do want to listen, but if I think I know the answer I kind of fire it off too soon.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience feelings of anxiety, worry, or irritability?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nI can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. But as far as anxiety or constant worry, I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, quiet stuff is hard for me if I’m supposed to just sit there. The clearest place it shows up is meetings, honestly. Everybody else can sit through the whole thing, and I’m the one standing up at some point, walking over to the coffee, stepping outside like I need to check something, or making some excuse to move around.\n\nI’ll try to make it look normal, like, “I’m just grabbing that printout,” or “Let me go check on that real quick,” but a lot of the time it’s not really about the coffee or the paper or whatever. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. I start feeling stuck, and then I’m looking for a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. through directions before I really understood them.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: At work it has gotten bad enough that missed follow-ups and poorly organized site coordination led to formal concern from my employer, and at home my wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I said I would handle.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: It has been seriously disrupting my job for about two years and two months, ever since the construction schedule changed and the hours got more chaotic.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 40 / construction_manager / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S002\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, this has been around as long as I can remember. It’s not like it showed up in adulthood or after I started working. Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents about it.\n\nI remember — well, some of it I remember and some of it is from my parents repeating it over the years — but they’d get told I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always popping up or fidgeting around, and that I talked out of turn. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.\n\nThe directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know if I’d describe it as an internal engine or anything like that. Like, I can be restless sometimes, but whether that’s a “strong need to be active” even when I’m sitting still… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it in that way.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, even when I’m trying to.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so I’m in safety briefings and plan reviews all the time, and I’ll go in telling myself, like, “Okay, pay attention, this matters.” And I do mean to. But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’m trying hard to focus, especially on safety stuff, but my mind just keeps jumping off track. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only at work or do you find it happening in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worst and most obvious at work, because that’s where it has consequences right away, you know? Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're not at work, like when you're reading, watching TV, or having conversations with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is hard for me, especially in meetings. Like, if everybody else can just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy who’s up at some point — getting coffee, stepping out to “check something,” walking over to look at a printout, anything that gives me a reason to move.\n\nSometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until afterward, but other times I’m sitting there and it feels like I’ve got to get up or I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll make it look like I’m being productive, like, “Oh, I’m just going to check on that,” or “Let me grab something,” but honestly a lot of it is just needing to not be stuck in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially with talking. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like I cut them off, gave this whole answer, and then they say, “No, what I meant was…” or they finish the sentence and it’s not exactly what I thought. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out fast, like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nIt happens enough that people will pause and look at me, or I’ll have to say, “Sorry, go ahead, finish what you were saying.” I hate that, because I do want to listen, but if I think I know the answer I kind of fire it off too soon.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience feelings of anxiety, worry, or irritability?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nI can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. But as far as anxiety or constant worry, I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, quiet stuff is hard for me if I’m supposed to just sit there. The clearest place it shows up is meetings, honestly. Everybody else can sit through the whole thing, and I’m the one standing up at some point, walking over to the coffee, stepping outside like I need to check something, or making some excuse to move around.\n\nI’ll try to make it look normal, like, “I’m just grabbing that printout,” or “Let me go check on that real quick,” but a lot of the time it’s not really about the coffee or the paper or whatever. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. I start feeling stuck, and then I’m looking for a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, this has been around as long as I can remember. It’s not like it showed up in adulthood or after I started working. Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents about it.\n\nI remember — well, some of it I remember and some of it is from my parents repeating it over the years — but they’d get told I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always popping up or fidgeting around, and that I talked out of turn. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.\n\nThe directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know if I’d describe it as an internal engine or anything like that. Like, I can be restless sometimes, but whether that’s a “strong need to be active” even when I’m sitting still… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it in that way.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. If you mean do I feel uncomfortable just doing nothing, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking. It’s hard for me to separate what’s inside from just normal stress or being used to being busy all the time, especially with my kind of work. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m sure, because I’m not really sure.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, this has been around as long as I can remember. It’s not like it showed up in adulthood or after I started working. Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents about it.\n\nI remember — well, some of it I remember and some of it is from my parents repeating it over the years — but they’d get told I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always popping up or fidgeting around, and that I talked out of turn. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.\n\nThe directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know, that one’s a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t automatically say I feel like I have “extra energy” in the sense of, like, I wake up and feel charged up and need to go run laps or something. That’s not really how I think about it.

I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nI can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.\n\nThe directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know if I’d describe it as an internal engine or anything like that. Like, I can be restless sometimes, but whether that’s a “strong need to be active” even when I’m sitting still… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it in that way.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. If you mean do I feel uncomfortable just doing nothing, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking. It’s hard for me to separate what’s inside from just normal stress or being used to being busy all the time, especially with my kind of work. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m sure, because I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, that one’s a little hard for me to pin down. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. I haven’t taken a formal leave of absence, no. I’ve kept working, but… it has gotten close to the point where it feels like work is noticing in a serious way.

At my job, if I miss a follow-up, it’s not just an email sitting there — it can mess up the whole site coordination. Like one trade is waiting on an answer, or something doesn’t get passed along clearly, or I said I’d circle back and then it slips, and suddenly people are standing around or the schedule gets messy. That’s happened enough that my employer has raised formal concern about it, which was pretty embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I just don’t care, because I do care, but things fall through unless I’m constantly chasing them.

And at home it’s been hard too. My wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. It’s not one big dramatic thing, it’s the constant stuff — I say I’ll take care of something, then she has to ask again, then remind me again, and by that point she’s frustrated because it feels like she’s managing me. So I haven’t been unable to work completely, but it’s definitely caused real problems at work and at home.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, even when I’m trying to.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so I’m in safety briefings and plan reviews all the time, and I’ll go in telling myself, like, “Okay, pay attention, this matters.” And I do mean to. But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. I’m trying hard to focus, especially on safety stuff, but my mind just keeps jumping off track. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Does this difficulty with focusing happen only at work or do you find it happening in other parts of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely worst and most obvious at work, because that’s where it has consequences right away, you know? Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're not at work, like when you're reading, watching TV, or having conversations with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is hard for me, especially in meetings. Like, if everybody else can just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy who’s up at some point — getting coffee, stepping out to “check something,” walking over to look at a printout, anything that gives me a reason to move.\n\nSometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until afterward, but other times I’m sitting there and it feels like I’ve got to get up or I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll make it look like I’m being productive, like, “Oh, I’m just going to check on that,” or “Let me grab something,” but honestly a lot of it is just needing to not be stuck in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially with talking. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like I cut them off, gave this whole answer, and then they say, “No, what I meant was…” or they finish the sentence and it’s not exactly what I thought. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out fast, like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nIt happens enough that people will pause and look at me, or I’ll have to say, “Sorry, go ahead, finish what you were saying.” I hate that, because I do want to listen, but if I think I know the answer I kind of fire it off too soon.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience feelings of anxiety, worry, or irritability?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nI can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. But as far as anxiety or constant worry, I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, quiet stuff is hard for me if I’m supposed to just sit there. The clearest place it shows up is meetings, honestly. Everybody else can sit through the whole thing, and I’m the one standing up at some point, walking over to the coffee, stepping outside like I need to check something, or making some excuse to move around.\n\nI’ll try to make it look normal, like, “I’m just grabbing that printout,” or “Let me go check on that real quick,” but a lot of the time it’s not really about the coffee or the paper or whatever. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. I start feeling stuck, and then I’m looking for a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, this has been around as long as I can remember. It’s not like it showed up in adulthood or after I started working. Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents about it.\n\nI remember — well, some of it I remember and some of it is from my parents repeating it over the years — but they’d get told I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always popping up or fidgeting around, and that I talked out of turn. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.\n\nThe directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know if I’d describe it as an internal engine or anything like that. Like, I can be restless sometimes, but whether that’s a “strong need to be active” even when I’m sitting still… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it in that way.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. If you mean do I feel uncomfortable just doing nothing, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking. It’s hard for me to separate what’s inside from just normal stress or being used to being busy all the time, especially with my kind of work. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m sure, because I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, that one’s a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t automatically say I feel like I have “extra energy” in the sense of, like, I wake up and feel charged up and need to go run laps or something. That’s not really how I think about it.\n\nDuring the day I’m usually moving around because of work anyway, or there’s always something that needs doing, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s me having energy to burn off versus just being used to being on the go. If I sit for a while, sometimes I get antsy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you’re asking.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing, like “I have too much energy and need to get it out.” It’s more like… I don’t know, I’m just not great at doing nothing. But I’m not sure I’d describe that as extra energy exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or had to take a leave of absence due to your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t taken a formal leave of absence, no. I’ve kept working, but… it has gotten close to the point where it feels like work is noticing in a serious way.\n\nAt my job, if I miss a follow-up, it’s not just an email sitting there — it can mess up the whole site coordination. Like one trade is waiting on an answer, or something doesn’t get passed along clearly, or I said I’d circle back and then it slips, and suddenly people are standing around or the schedule gets messy. That’s happened enough that my employer has raised formal concern about it, which was pretty embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I just don’t care, because I do care, but things fall through unless I’m constantly chasing them.\n\nAnd at home it’s been hard too. My wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. It’s not one big dramatic thing, it’s the constant stuff — I say I’ll take care of something, then she has to ask again, then remind me again, and by that point she’s frustrated because it feels like she’s managing me. So I haven’t been unable to work completely, but it’s definitely caused real problems at work and at home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

answer too fast and sometimes realize I missed the important part they hadn’t said yet.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn on a call, at the permit office, or in a line drives me nuts, and I’m checking the time, shifting around, and fighting the urge to push ahead.\n- key: S018\n  name: often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know if I’d describe it as an internal engine or anything like that. Like, I can be restless sometimes, but whether that’s a “strong need to be active” even when I’m sitting still… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it in that way.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m sure, because I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, that one’s a little hard for me to pin down. Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying “couldn’t overcome,” because I always feel like I should be able to just tighten up and fix it, you know? But realistically, yes, it’s caused problems that I haven’t been able to just push through.

One person is waiting on an answer, another trade doesn’t know what’s happening, something I said I’d circle back on doesn’t happen, and then it turns into a bigger issue. I’ll have every intention of handling it, but if I don’t keep on top of every single piece, something slips. And when that happens enough, people start losing confidence that I’m going to stay on top of things. That’s been a real problem.

And at home, it’s been hard on my wife. She’s tired of having to remind me about things I already told her I’d handle. It’s not like one big argument and then it’s over — it’s the repeated reminders, the same pattern, where she feels like she has to manage me. I can tell it wears her down, and honestly it makes me feel pretty bad, because I’m not trying to dump things on her. But it keeps happening. So yes, between work and home, it’s definitely caused significant problems.
