I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, this has been around as long as I can remember. It’s not like it showed up in adulthood or after I started working. Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents about it.\n\nI remember — well, some of it I remember and some of it is from my parents repeating it over the years — but they’d get told I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always popping up or fidgeting around, and that I talked out of turn. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.\n\nThe directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know if I’d describe it as an internal engine or anything like that. Like, I can be restless sometimes, but whether that’s a “strong need to be active” even when I’m sitting still… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it in that way.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. If you mean do I feel uncomfortable just doing nothing, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking. It’s hard for me to separate what’s inside from just normal stress or being used to being busy all the time, especially with my kind of work. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m sure, because I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, that one’s a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t automatically say I feel like I have “extra energy” in the sense of, like, I wake up and feel charged up and need to go run laps or something. That’s not really how I think about it.\n\nDuring the day I’m usually moving around because of work anyway, or there’s always something that needs doing, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s me having energy to burn off versus just being used to being on the go. If I sit for a while, sometimes I get antsy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you’re asking.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing, like “I have too much energy and need to get it out.” It’s more like… I don’t know, I’m just not great at doing nothing. But I’m not sure I’d describe that as extra energy exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or had to take a leave of absence due to your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t taken a formal leave of absence, no. I’ve kept working, but… it has gotten close to the point where it feels like work is noticing in a serious way.\n\nAt my job, if I miss a follow-up, it’s not just an email sitting there — it can mess up the whole site coordination. Like one trade is waiting on an answer, or something doesn’t get passed along clearly, or I said I’d circle back and then it slips, and suddenly people are standing around or the schedule gets messy. That’s happened enough that my employer has raised formal concern about it, which was pretty embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I just don’t care, because I do care, but things fall through unless I’m constantly chasing them.\n\nAnd at home it’s been hard too. My wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. It’s not one big dramatic thing, it’s the constant stuff — I say I’ll take care of something, then she has to ask again, then remind me again, and by that point she’s frustrated because it feels like she’s managing me. So I haven’t been unable to work completely, but it’s definitely caused real problems at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness led to any significant problems in your personal relationships or at work that you couldn't overcome?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying “couldn’t overcome,” because I always feel like I should be able to just tighten up and fix it, you know? But realistically, yes, it’s caused problems that I haven’t been able to just push through.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally brought it up. In construction, if I miss a follow-up or I don’t organize the site coordination clearly, it’s not just a small thing. One person is waiting on an answer, another trade doesn’t know what’s happening, something I said I’d circle back on doesn’t happen, and then it turns into a bigger issue. I’ll have every intention of handling it, but if I don’t keep on top of every single piece, something slips. And when that happens enough, people start losing confidence that I’m going to stay on top of things. That’s been a real problem.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been hard on my wife. She’s tired of having to remind me about things I already told her I’d handle. It’s not like one big argument and then it’s over — it’s the repeated reminders, the same pattern, where she feels like she has to manage me. I can tell it wears her down, and honestly it makes me feel pretty bad, because I’m not trying to dump things on her. But it keeps happening. So yes, between work and home, it’s definitely caused significant problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness consistently throughout your life, since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it goes way back. I mean, I didn’t have words for it as a kid, obviously, but from what I remember — and from what my parents used to tell me — it was already showing up in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was talking when I wasn’t supposed to, blurting things out, that kind of thing. And homework was always a problem — not always because I didn’t do it, but because I’d lose it or forget to turn it in, or it would be in my backpack crumpled up after my mom had already helped me with it. I also remember rushing through directions before I actually understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear the first part, think I got it, and just start, and then it would turn out I missed half of what the teacher said.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent. It wasn’t like this just started as an adult with work stress. The consequences are bigger now, obviously, but the same kind of pattern was there when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I’ve never been formally diagnosed with anything like that before.\n\nAs far as family… I’m honestly not sure. Nobody in my family has ever really talked about it in those terms, at least not that I remember. I don’t know if that’s because nobody had it, or because when I was growing up people just didn’t label things that way. So I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound like I know for sure. I just don’t have a clear family history that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness have caused more problems in your life than your fidgeting or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say the focus and follow-through problems have caused way more damage than just the fidgeting or restlessness by itself.\n\nI mean, the restlessness is annoying, and I’m sure people notice it sometimes, but the bigger consequences have been when I don’t keep track of things or I fail to follow up. At work, that’s where it’s really shown up. If I miss a follow-up or I don’t organize the site coordination clearly, it can throw other people off, and then it becomes a bigger issue than I meant it to be. It’s gotten to the point where my employer has formally raised concerns about it, which is hard to admit. That feels like more than just being restless.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My wife isn’t worn down because I bounce my leg or can’t sit still — she’s worn down because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then she has to remind me again and again. It puts her in that position of managing me, and I know that’s frustrating for her. So yeah, the fidgeting is there, but the real problems have been from not staying on top of things and not following through.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness as a child, to the point where it caused problems in school or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that was already happening when I was pretty young, like in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, and that I’d talk out of turn or blurt things out when I was supposed to be quiet. And homework was a whole issue too — I’d lose it, or it wouldn’t make it back to the teacher even if it was done. I remember that being a repeated thing, not just once in a while.\n\nAnd with directions, I had this habit of rushing ahead before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear part of the instructions and just start, and then I’d find out I missed something important. So yeah, it caused problems at school for sure, and at home too because my parents were constantly hearing about it or having to deal with the homework stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, they did, at least in the way they described it back then. I don’t remember them using words like “time management” exactly, but the problems were definitely there.

Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I talked out of turn, and that I was losing homework or not turning it in. It wasn’t just me being a little messy one time — it was a repeated thing. Homework would get done and then somehow not make it back, or I’d lose the paper, or I’d rush through what I was supposed to do before I really understood the directions.

That was a big one, actually — I’d hear the first part of the instructions and just start doing it, like I was trying to get ahead, but then I’d miss something important and have to redo it or get in trouble. So, yeah, looking back, I think that was part of the organization problem, even if nobody called it that. My parents definitely heard about it from teachers more than once.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess what finally made me come in is that I’m having a really hard time staying with things mentally, even when I’m trying to.\n\nI’m a construction manager, so I’m in safety briefings and plan reviews all the time, and I’ll go in telling myself, like, “Okay, pay attention, this matters.” And I do mean to. But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're not at work, like when you're reading, watching TV, or having conversations with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is hard for me, especially in meetings. Like, if everybody else can just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy who’s up at some point — getting coffee, stepping out to “check something,” walking over to look at a printout, anything that gives me a reason to move.\n\nSometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until afterward, but other times I’m sitting there and it feels like I’ve got to get up or I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll make it look like I’m being productive, like, “Oh, I’m just going to check on that,” or “Let me grab something,” but honestly a lot of it is just needing to not be stuck in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially with talking. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like I cut them off, gave this whole answer, and then they say, “No, what I meant was…” or they finish the sentence and it’s not exactly what I thought. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out fast, like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nIt happens enough that people will pause and look at me, or I’ll have to say, “Sorry, go ahead, finish what you were saying.” I hate that, because I do want to listen, but if I think I know the answer I kind of fire it off too soon.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience feelings of anxiety, worry, or irritability?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nI can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. But as far as anxiety or constant worry, I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, quiet stuff is hard for me if I’m supposed to just sit there. The clearest place it shows up is meetings, honestly. Everybody else can sit through the whole thing, and I’m the one standing up at some point, walking over to the coffee, stepping outside like I need to check something, or making some excuse to move around.\n\nI’ll try to make it look normal, like, “I’m just grabbing that printout,” or “Let me go check on that real quick,” but a lot of the time it’s not really about the coffee or the paper or whatever. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. I start feeling stuck, and then I’m looking for a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, this has been around as long as I can remember. It’s not like it showed up in adulthood or after I started working. Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents about it.\n\nI remember — well, some of it I remember and some of it is from my parents repeating it over the years — but they’d get told I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always popping up or fidgeting around, and that I talked out of turn. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.\n\nThe directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know if I’d describe it as an internal engine or anything like that. Like, I can be restless sometimes, but whether that’s a “strong need to be active” even when I’m sitting still… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it in that way.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. If you mean do I feel uncomfortable just doing nothing, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking. It’s hard for me to separate what’s inside from just normal stress or being used to being busy all the time, especially with my kind of work. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m sure, because I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, that one’s a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t automatically say I feel like I have “extra energy” in the sense of, like, I wake up and feel charged up and need to go run laps or something. That’s not really how I think about it.\n\nDuring the day I’m usually moving around because of work anyway, or there’s always something that needs doing, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s me having energy to burn off versus just being used to being on the go. If I sit for a while, sometimes I get antsy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you’re asking.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing, like “I have too much energy and need to get it out.” It’s more like… I don’t know, I’m just not great at doing nothing. But I’m not sure I’d describe that as extra energy exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or had to take a leave of absence due to your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t taken a formal leave of absence, no. I’ve kept working, but… it has gotten close to the point where it feels like work is noticing in a serious way.\n\nAt my job, if I miss a follow-up, it’s not just an email sitting there — it can mess up the whole site coordination. Like one trade is waiting on an answer, or something doesn’t get passed along clearly, or I said I’d circle back and then it slips, and suddenly people are standing around or the schedule gets messy. That’s happened enough that my employer has raised formal concern about it, which was pretty embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I just don’t care, because I do care, but things fall through unless I’m constantly chasing them.\n\nAnd at home it’s been hard too. My wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. It’s not one big dramatic thing, it’s the constant stuff — I say I’ll take care of something, then she has to ask again, then remind me again, and by that point she’s frustrated because it feels like she’s managing me. So I haven’t been unable to work completely, but it’s definitely caused real problems at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness led to any significant problems in your personal relationships or at work that you couldn't overcome?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying “couldn’t overcome,” because I always feel like I should be able to just tighten up and fix it, you know? But realistically, yes, it’s caused problems that I haven’t been able to just push through.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally brought it up. In construction, if I miss a follow-up or I don’t organize the site coordination clearly, it’s not just a small thing. One person is waiting on an answer, another trade doesn’t know what’s happening, something I said I’d circle back on doesn’t happen, and then it turns into a bigger issue. I’ll have every intention of handling it, but if I don’t keep on top of every single piece, something slips. And when that happens enough, people start losing confidence that I’m going to stay on top of things. That’s been a real problem.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been hard on my wife. She’s tired of having to remind me about things I already told her I’d handle. It’s not like one big argument and then it’s over — it’s the repeated reminders, the same pattern, where she feels like she has to manage me. I can tell it wears her down, and honestly it makes me feel pretty bad, because I’m not trying to dump things on her. But it keeps happening. So yes, between work and home, it’s definitely caused significant problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness consistently throughout your life, since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it goes way back. I mean, I didn’t have words for it as a kid, obviously, but from what I remember — and from what my parents used to tell me — it was already showing up in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was talking when I wasn’t supposed to, blurting things out, that kind of thing. And homework was always a problem — not always because I didn’t do it, but because I’d lose it or forget to turn it in, or it would be in my backpack crumpled up after my mom had already helped me with it. I also remember rushing through directions before I actually understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear the first part, think I got it, and just start, and then it would turn out I missed half of what the teacher said.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent. It wasn’t like this just started as an adult with work stress. The consequences are bigger now, obviously, but the same kind of pattern was there when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I’ve never been formally diagnosed with anything like that before.\n\nAs far as family… I’m honestly not sure. Nobody in my family has ever really talked about it in those terms, at least not that I remember. I don’t know if that’s because nobody had it, or because when I was growing up people just didn’t label things that way. So I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound like I know for sure. I just don’t have a clear family history that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness have caused more problems in your life than your fidgeting or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say the focus and follow-through problems have caused way more damage than just the fidgeting or restlessness by itself.\n\nI mean, the restlessness is annoying, and I’m sure people notice it sometimes, but the bigger consequences have been when I don’t keep track of things or I fail to follow up. At work, that’s where it’s really shown up. If I miss a follow-up or I don’t organize the site coordination clearly, it can throw other people off, and then it becomes a bigger issue than I meant it to be. It’s gotten to the point where my employer has formally raised concerns about it, which is hard to admit. That feels like more than just being restless.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My wife isn’t worn down because I bounce my leg or can’t sit still — she’s worn down because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then she has to remind me again and again. It puts her in that position of managing me, and I know that’s frustrating for her. So yeah, the fidgeting is there, but the real problems have been from not staying on top of things and not following through.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness as a child, to the point where it caused problems in school or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that was already happening when I was pretty young, like in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, and that I’d talk out of turn or blurt things out when I was supposed to be quiet. And homework was a whole issue too — I’d lose it, or it wouldn’t make it back to the teacher even if it was done. I remember that being a repeated thing, not just once in a while.\n\nAnd with directions, I had this habit of rushing ahead before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear part of the instructions and just start, and then I’d find out I missed something important. So yeah, it caused problems at school for sure, and at home too because my parents were constantly hearing about it or having to deal with the homework stuff.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had significant problems with organization or time management when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did, at least in the way they described it back then. I don’t remember them using words like “time management” exactly, but the problems were definitely there.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I talked out of turn, and that I was losing homework or not turning it in. It wasn’t just me being a little messy one time — it was a repeated thing. Homework would get done and then somehow not make it back, or I’d lose the paper, or I’d rush through what I was supposed to do before I really understood the directions.\n\nThat was a big one, actually — I’d hear the first part of the instructions and just start doing it, like I was trying to get ahead, but then I’d miss something important and have to redo it or get in trouble. So, yeah, looking back, I think that was part of the organization problem, even if nobody called it that. My parents definitely heard about it from teachers more than once.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can tell it wears her down, and honestly it makes me feel pretty bad, because I’m not trying to dump things on her. But it keeps happening. So yes, between work and home, it’s definitely caused significant problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness consistently throughout your life, since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it goes way back. I mean, I didn’t have words for it as a kid, obviously, but from what I remember — and from what my parents used to tell me — it was already showing up in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was talking when I wasn’t supposed to, blurting things out, that kind of thing. And homework was always a problem — not always because I didn’t do it, but because I’d lose it or forget to turn it in, or it would be in my backpack crumpled up after my mom had already helped me with it. I also remember rushing through directions before I actually understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear the first part, think I got it, and just start, and then it would turn out I missed half of what the teacher said.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent. It wasn’t like this just started as an adult with work stress. The consequences are bigger now, obviously, but the same kind of pattern was there when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I’ve never been formally diagnosed with anything like that before.\n\nAs far as family… I’m honestly not sure. Nobody in my family has ever really talked about it in those terms, at least not that I remember. I don’t know if that’s because nobody had it, or because when I was growing up people just didn’t label things that way. So I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound like I know for sure. I just don’t have a clear family history that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness have caused more problems in your life than your fidgeting or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say the focus and follow-through problems have caused way more damage than just the fidgeting or restlessness by itself.\n\nI mean, the restlessness is annoying, and I’m sure people notice it sometimes, but the bigger consequences have been when I don’t keep track of things or I fail to follow up. At work, that’s where it’s really shown up. If I miss a follow-up or I don’t organize the site coordination clearly, it can throw other people off, and then it becomes a bigger issue than I meant it to be. It’s gotten to the point where my employer has formally raised concerns about it, which is hard to admit. That feels like more than just being restless.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My wife isn’t worn down because I bounce my leg or can’t sit still — she’s worn down because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then she has to remind me again and again. It puts her in that position of managing me, and I know that’s frustrating for her. So yeah, the fidgeting is there, but the real problems have been from not staying on top of things and not following through.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness as a child, to the point where it caused problems in school or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that was already happening when I was pretty young, like in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, and that I’d talk out of turn or blurt things out when I was supposed to be quiet. And homework was a whole issue too — I’d lose it, or it wouldn’t make it back to the teacher even if it was done. I remember that being a repeated thing, not just once in a while.\n\nAnd with directions, I had this habit of rushing ahead before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear part of the instructions and just start, and then I’d find out I missed something important. So yeah, it caused problems at school for sure, and at home too because my parents were constantly hearing about it or having to deal with the homework stuff.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had significant problems with organization or time management when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did, at least in the way they described it back then. Yeah, it did. I mean, especially academically, that was where it showed up the most when I was a kid.

Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents things like I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I was talking out of turn when I was supposed to be quiet. Then I’d miss some important part and either get it wrong or have to redo it. So it wasn’t just me being “energetic” or whatever — it actually caused problems in school, and then of course it became a problem at home because my parents were hearing about it from teachers.

But within a few minutes, my head is already somewhere else — thinking about three other job problems, a subcontractor issue, some schedule thing, whatever — and then I realize I missed part of what was just said or something on the plans that I really should’ve caught.\n\nIt’s frustrating because it’s not like I don’t care. Like in a safety briefing or a plan review, I’ll go in really intending to listen, and I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Don’t miss this, this matters,” and then a few minutes later my mind has gone off to three other job problems — a subcontractor issue, something with the schedule, some thing I need to check later — and then I kind of come back and realize I missed part of what was said.\n\nThat’s the part that bothers me. It’s not like I’m bored on purpose or blowing it off. I can be trying hard to pay attention and still miss things I know I should’ve caught. So, I guess work is where I notice it the most because it’s constant there.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing when you're not at work, like when you're reading, watching TV, or having conversations with friends and family?\n\nPatient: I mean, yeah, I guess it does happen outside of work too, but the clearest example for me is still the work stuff because that’s where I can really point to it and say, “Okay, I missed that.” Like I’ll start a safety briefing or sit down for a plan review honestly trying hard to pay attention — not half-listening, not messing around — and within a few minutes my mind is already off somewhere else, like thinking about three other job problems at the same time.\n\nAnd then I’ll sort of snap back and realize I missed something that was just said, or missed a detail on the plans that I know I should’ve caught. That’s the part that makes me feel stupid, honestly, because I’m trying to focus. It’s not that I don’t care. It just feels like I can’t keep my attention locked on the thing in front of me for very long.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. Sitting still is hard for me, especially in meetings. Like, if everybody else can just sit there through the whole thing, I’m usually the guy who’s up at some point — getting coffee, stepping out to “check something,” walking over to look at a printout, anything that gives me a reason to move.\n\nSometimes I don’t even realize I’m doing it until afterward, but other times I’m sitting there and it feels like I’ve got to get up or I’m going to crawl out of my skin. I’ll make it look like I’m being productive, like, “Oh, I’m just going to check on that,” or “Let me grab something,” but honestly a lot of it is just needing to not be stuck in the chair.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, especially with talking. I’ll jump in before somebody’s even finished asking me something, because in my head I’m already like, “I know where this is going,” and I answer the question I think they’re asking.\n\nAnd then sometimes, a second later, I realize they hadn’t actually gotten to the important part yet. Like I cut them off, gave this whole answer, and then they say, “No, what I meant was…” or they finish the sentence and it’s not exactly what I thought. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be rude. It just comes out fast, like my mouth gets ahead of the rest of me.\n\nIt happens enough that people will pause and look at me, or I’ll have to say, “Sorry, go ahead, finish what you were saying.” I hate that, because I do want to listen, but if I think I know the answer I kind of fire it off too soon.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience feelings of anxiety, worry, or irritability?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I get irritated sometimes, sure, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxiety exactly. Like, I’m not sitting around thinking, “I’m worried about this or that” all day, at least not in a way I’ve really paid attention to.\n\nI can get wound up when things aren’t moving or when I’m stuck waiting, and I probably come off short with people more than I mean to. But as far as anxiety or constant worry, I’m not sure. I guess I haven’t really thought about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or have trouble engaging in quiet leisure activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, quiet stuff is hard for me if I’m supposed to just sit there. The clearest place it shows up is meetings, honestly. Everybody else can sit through the whole thing, and I’m the one standing up at some point, walking over to the coffee, stepping outside like I need to check something, or making some excuse to move around.\n\nI’ll try to make it look normal, like, “I’m just grabbing that printout,” or “Let me go check on that real quick,” but a lot of the time it’s not really about the coffee or the paper or whatever. It’s just… I can’t stay parked in the chair like everyone else. I start feeling stuck, and then I’m looking for a reason to get up.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends and family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, it definitely has. I mean, at home especially, my wife is just tired. Not like annoyed for five minutes — I mean worn down from having to keep reminding me about things I already told her I’d take care of. Like I’ll say, “Yeah, I’ve got it, I’ll handle it,” and then later she’s asking me again because I didn’t follow through, or I only did part of it, or I forgot some piece of it. And from her side, I’m sure it feels like she has to manage me, which is not fair to her.\n\nAnd work has been a problem too. I’m a construction manager, so there’s a lot of moving parts, and if I miss a follow-up or don’t keep the site coordination organized, it doesn’t just affect me — it affects other people waiting on answers or direction. It’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally raised concerns about it. That was… honestly, pretty embarrassing. It wasn’t just someone casually saying, “Hey, get it together.” It was more serious than that.\n\nSo yeah, it’s not just in my head or something I notice privately. It’s affecting people around me, and I know they’re getting frustrated with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself so focused on something that you tune out everything else around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I know people talk about getting “locked in” on something, but I don’t know that I’ve really noticed that in myself in a clear way.\n\nLike, there are probably times where I’m doing something and I’m not paying attention to someone talking to me, but I don’t know if that’s because I’m so focused or because my mind is somewhere else, if that makes sense. I wouldn’t want to say, “Oh yeah, I tune everything out,” because I’m not sure that’s really what’s happening.\n\nCan you say what you mean by that a little more? Like, do you mean if I’m working on a task and I don’t hear people, or more like I can’t stop doing something once I start?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had these difficulties with focus and impulsivity, or did they start at some point in your life?\n\nPatient: No, this has been around as long as I can remember. It’s not like it showed up in adulthood or after I started working. Back in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents about it.\n\nI remember — well, some of it I remember and some of it is from my parents repeating it over the years — but they’d get told I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was always popping up or fidgeting around, and that I talked out of turn. Like I’d answer before I was supposed to, or interrupt, or just blurt things out. And homework was a whole thing too. I’d lose it, forget to turn it in, or have it shoved somewhere in my backpack and not know where it went.\n\nThe directions part was big too. Teachers would say I rushed through instructions before I really understood what they were asking. I’d hear the first part and just start doing it, and then later it turned out I’d missed half the directions. So… yeah, it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel an internal sense of overactivity or a strong need to be active, even when you're not necessarily fidgeting or moving around?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t know if I’d describe it as an internal engine or anything like that. Like, I can be restless sometimes, but whether that’s a “strong need to be active” even when I’m sitting still… I don’t know that I’ve paid attention to it in that way.\n\nI guess I’d have to think about what counts. If you mean do I feel uncomfortable just doing nothing, maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you’re asking. It’s hard for me to separate what’s inside from just normal stress or being used to being busy all the time, especially with my kind of work. So I don’t want to say yes like I’m sure, because I’m not really sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have a lot of extra energy that you need to burn off during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, that one’s a little hard for me to pin down. I wouldn’t automatically say I feel like I have “extra energy” in the sense of, like, I wake up and feel charged up and need to go run laps or something. That’s not really how I think about it.\n\nDuring the day I’m usually moving around because of work anyway, or there’s always something that needs doing, so it’s hard for me to tell what’s me having energy to burn off versus just being used to being on the go. If I sit for a while, sometimes I get antsy, but I don’t know if that’s the same as what you’re asking.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing, like “I have too much energy and need to get it out.” It’s more like… I don’t know, I’m just not great at doing nothing. But I’m not sure I’d describe that as extra energy exactly.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to work or had to take a leave of absence due to your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness?\n\nPatient: I haven’t taken a formal leave of absence, no. I’ve kept working, but… it has gotten close to the point where it feels like work is noticing in a serious way.\n\nAt my job, if I miss a follow-up, it’s not just an email sitting there — it can mess up the whole site coordination. Like one trade is waiting on an answer, or something doesn’t get passed along clearly, or I said I’d circle back and then it slips, and suddenly people are standing around or the schedule gets messy. That’s happened enough that my employer has raised formal concern about it, which was pretty embarrassing, honestly. I don’t want to make it sound like I just don’t care, because I do care, but things fall through unless I’m constantly chasing them.\n\nAnd at home it’s been hard too. My wife is worn down from having to remind me about things I already said I’d handle. It’s not one big dramatic thing, it’s the constant stuff — I say I’ll take care of something, then she has to ask again, then remind me again, and by that point she’s frustrated because it feels like she’s managing me. So I haven’t been unable to work completely, but it’s definitely caused real problems at work and at home.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness led to any significant problems in your personal relationships or at work that you couldn't overcome?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t like saying “couldn’t overcome,” because I always feel like I should be able to just tighten up and fix it, you know? But realistically, yes, it’s caused problems that I haven’t been able to just push through.\n\nAt work, it’s gotten bad enough that my employer has formally brought it up. In construction, if I miss a follow-up or I don’t organize the site coordination clearly, it’s not just a small thing. One person is waiting on an answer, another trade doesn’t know what’s happening, something I said I’d circle back on doesn’t happen, and then it turns into a bigger issue. I’ll have every intention of handling it, but if I don’t keep on top of every single piece, something slips. And when that happens enough, people start losing confidence that I’m going to stay on top of things. That’s been a real problem.\n\nAnd at home, it’s been hard on my wife. She’s tired of having to remind me about things I already told her I’d handle. It’s not like one big argument and then it’s over — it’s the repeated reminders, the same pattern, where she feels like she has to manage me. I can tell it wears her down, and honestly it makes me feel pretty bad, because I’m not trying to dump things on her. But it keeps happening. So yes, between work and home, it’s definitely caused significant problems.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness consistently throughout your life, since you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it goes way back. I mean, I didn’t have words for it as a kid, obviously, but from what I remember — and from what my parents used to tell me — it was already showing up in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I was talking when I wasn’t supposed to, blurting things out, that kind of thing. And homework was always a problem — not always because I didn’t do it, but because I’d lose it or forget to turn it in, or it would be in my backpack crumpled up after my mom had already helped me with it. I also remember rushing through directions before I actually understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear the first part, think I got it, and just start, and then it would turn out I missed half of what the teacher said.\n\nSo, yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent. It wasn’t like this just started as an adult with work stress. The consequences are bigger now, obviously, but the same kind of pattern was there when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention deficit hyperactivity disorder or does anyone in your family have it?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I’ve never been formally diagnosed with anything like that before.\n\nAs far as family… I’m honestly not sure. Nobody in my family has ever really talked about it in those terms, at least not that I remember. I don’t know if that’s because nobody had it, or because when I was growing up people just didn’t label things that way. So I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound like I know for sure. I just don’t have a clear family history that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness have caused more problems in your life than your fidgeting or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say the focus and follow-through problems have caused way more damage than just the fidgeting or restlessness by itself.\n\nI mean, the restlessness is annoying, and I’m sure people notice it sometimes, but the bigger consequences have been when I don’t keep track of things or I fail to follow up. At work, that’s where it’s really shown up. If I miss a follow-up or I don’t organize the site coordination clearly, it can throw other people off, and then it becomes a bigger issue than I meant it to be. It’s gotten to the point where my employer has formally raised concerns about it, which is hard to admit. That feels like more than just being restless.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My wife isn’t worn down because I bounce my leg or can’t sit still — she’s worn down because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then she has to remind me again and again. It puts her in that position of managing me, and I know that’s frustrating for her. So yeah, the fidgeting is there, but the real problems have been from not staying on top of things and not following through.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness as a child, to the point where it caused problems in school or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that was already happening when I was pretty young, like in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, and that I’d talk out of turn or blurt things out when I was supposed to be quiet. And homework was a whole issue too — I’d lose it, or it wouldn’t make it back to the teacher even if it was done. I remember that being a repeated thing, not just once in a while.\n\nAnd with directions, I had this habit of rushing ahead before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear part of the instructions and just start, and then I’d find out I missed something important. So yeah, it caused problems at school for sure, and at home too because my parents were constantly hearing about it or having to deal with the homework stuff.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had significant problems with organization or time management when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did, at least in the way they described it back then. I don’t remember them using words like “time management” exactly, but the problems were definitely there.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I talked out of turn, and that I was losing homework or not turning it in. It wasn’t just me being a little messy one time — it was a repeated thing. Homework would get done and then somehow not make it back, or I’d lose the paper, or I’d rush through what I was supposed to do before I really understood the directions.\n\nThat was a big one, actually — I’d hear the first part of the instructions and just start doing it, like I was trying to get ahead, but then I’d miss something important and have to redo it or get in trouble. So, yeah, looking back, I think that was part of the organization problem, even if nobody called it that. My parents definitely heard about it from teachers more than once.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness significantly impact your academic or social functioning as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. I mean, especially academically, that was where it showed up the most when I was a kid.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents things like I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I was talking out of turn when I was supposed to be quiet. I don’t know if that’s because nobody had it, or because when I was growing up people just didn’t label things that way. So I wouldn’t want to say yes and make it sound like I know for sure. I just don’t have a clear family history that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness have caused more problems in your life than your fidgeting or restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say the focus and follow-through problems have caused way more damage than just the fidgeting or restlessness by itself.\n\nI mean, the restlessness is annoying, and I’m sure people notice it sometimes, but the bigger consequences have been when I don’t keep track of things or I fail to follow up. At work, that’s where it’s really shown up. If I miss a follow-up or I don’t organize the site coordination clearly, it can throw other people off, and then it becomes a bigger issue than I meant it to be. It’s gotten to the point where my employer has formally raised concerns about it, which is hard to admit. That feels like more than just being restless.\n\nAnd at home, it’s similar. My wife isn’t worn down because I bounce my leg or can’t sit still — she’s worn down because I’ll say I’m going to handle something, and then she has to remind me again and again. It puts her in that position of managing me, and I know that’s frustrating for her. So yeah, the fidgeting is there, but the real problems have been from not staying on top of things and not following through.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience significant difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness as a child, to the point where it caused problems in school or at home?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that was already happening when I was pretty young, like in elementary school.\n\nTeachers were telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, and that I’d talk out of turn or blurt things out when I was supposed to be quiet. And homework was a whole issue too — I’d lose it, or it wouldn’t make it back to the teacher even if it was done. I remember that being a repeated thing, not just once in a while.\n\nAnd with directions, I had this habit of rushing ahead before I really understood what I was supposed to do. Like I’d hear part of the instructions and just start, and then I’d find out I missed something important. So yeah, it caused problems at school for sure, and at home too because my parents were constantly hearing about it or having to deal with the homework stuff.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had significant problems with organization or time management when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they did, at least in the way they described it back then. I don’t remember them using words like “time management” exactly, but the problems were definitely there.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents I couldn’t stay in my seat, that I talked out of turn, and that I was losing homework or not turning it in. It wasn’t just me being a little messy one time — it was a repeated thing. Homework would get done and then somehow not make it back, or I’d lose the paper, or I’d rush through what I was supposed to do before I really understood the directions.\n\nThat was a big one, actually — I’d hear the first part of the instructions and just start doing it, like I was trying to get ahead, but then I’d miss something important and have to redo it or get in trouble. So, yeah, looking back, I think that was part of the organization problem, even if nobody called it that. My parents definitely heard about it from teachers more than once.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with focus, impulsivity, or restlessness significantly impact your academic or social functioning as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it did. I mean, especially academically, that was where it showed up the most when I was a kid.\n\nBack in elementary school, teachers were already telling my parents things like I couldn’t stay in my seat, or I was talking out of turn when I was supposed to be quiet. And the homework thing was a constant problem too — I’d lose it, or I’d do it and then somehow not turn it in, which sounds ridiculous, but that really happened a lot.\n\nI also remember rushing through directions before I actually understood them. Like I’d hear the first part, assume I knew what to do, and just start. Then I’d miss some important part and either get it wrong or have to redo it. So it wasn’t just me being “energetic” or whatever — it actually caused problems in school, and then of course it became a problem at home because my parents were hearing about it from teachers."
